Identify the CDQ block lengths required by the recursion, and pre-compute the corresponding FFT plans and transformed kernel blocks

Build the fixed CDQ operation list (leaf/direct/FFT blocks)

Parallel simulation:

Determine the number of available CPU threads
N_{\text{threads}}

Partition
n_{\text{sims}}
simulations among threads

for

each thread in parallel

do

Initialize a thread-specific random number generator and Poisson sampler

Allocate once the reusable work arrays
\lambda^{\mathrm{hist}}
,
Y
, and the FFT buffer, together with thread-local sums and sums of squares for all payoffs

for

each simulation assigned to the thread

do

Reset the reusable workspace and initialize the running summaries
N^{\pm}
,
S_{\mathrm{avg}}
,
S_{\max}
,
S_{\min}
, and the barrier flags

for

each pre-computed CDQ operation in execution order

do

if

the operation is a leaf at time
t

then

\lambda\leftarrow\max\{0,\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(t)+\lambda^{\mathrm{hist}}_{t}\}

Sample
X_{t}^{+},X_{t}^{-}\sim\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda)
independently

Set
Y_{t}\leftarrow X_{t}^{+}+\beta X_{t}^{-}

Update the running totals
N_{t}^{\pm}\leftarrow N_{t-1}^{\pm}+X_{t}^{\pm}
and the price

S_{t}\leftarrow S_{0}\exp\!\left(c_{\tau}(N_{t}^{+}-N_{t}^{-})-d_{\tau}N_{t}^{+}\right)

Update the running average, extrema, and barrier indicators

else

if

the block is small

then

Propagate the left-to-right convolution directly

else

Reuse the pre-computed FFT plan and transformed kernel block to convolve the left data with the kernel and add the contribution to the right half of
\lambda^{\mathrm{hist}}

end if

end if

end for

Compute payoffs for all option types from the path summaries

Update the thread-local payoff sums and sums of squares

end for

end for

Postprocessing:

Aggregate the thread-local sums and sums of squares across all threads

For each option type, compute the mean and 95% confidence interval from the aggregated statistics

return

A structure containing all option price estimates and their confidence intervals

4.2
Parameter-specific checks of the analytical assumptions

The weak-error analysis is stated under Assumption
3.4
and under
the bounded-strip Riccati regularity condition
3.5
. Since
these assumptions are not proved in full
generality in the present paper, we record here the parameter-specific checks
used for the numerical experiments. The purpose of this subsection is precisely
to show the reader that the reported experiments are run in parameter regimes
for which the relevant assumptions are numerically supported. These checks do
not constitute general proofs.

Admissible-strip check

For the rough-parameter sets used in the numerical section, we numerically
checked the line
\mathrm{Re}\ z=2
at maturity
T=1
. On the continuous side, solving
(
3.7
) at
z=2
gave finite values, with

h(1,2)\approx 0.999

for the parameter set

(\alpha,\gamma,\rho,\nu,\theta,V_{0})=(0.62,0.1,-0.681,0.331,0.3156,0.0392),

and

h(1,2)\approx 0.688,\ 0.697,\ 0.715,\ 0.721

for the slice-comparison parameter set

(\gamma,\rho,\nu,\theta,V_{0},T)=(0.3,-0.7,1,0.02/0.3,0.02,1),

at
\alpha=0.55,0.62,0.80,0.95
. On the discrete side, evaluating
(
3.15
)–(
3.16
) at
z=2
yielded finite transforms
for the discretization levels used in the experiments:

\log\Phi^{\tau}(2,1)\approx 0.05593,\ 0.05574,\ 0.05564,\ 0.05560

for
\tau=40,80,160,320
in the first parameter set, and

\log\Phi^{160}(2,1)\approx 0.02321,\ 0.02307,\ 0.02276,\ 0.02254

for
\alpha=0.55,0.62,0.80,0.95
in the slice-comparison set. These
computations provide parameter-specific evidence that the line
\mathrm{Re}\ z=2
is
admissible for the reported experiments, but they are not a general proof of
Assumption
3.4
.

Bounded-strip Riccati check

The following computations verify Assumption
3.5
(the bounded-strip Riccati regularity condition stated in Section
3
) for the parameter sets used in the experiments.
For the same parameter sets, we numerically solved the fractional Riccati
equation on a grid of times
t\in[0,T]
and frequencies

z=\eta+\mathrm{i}\xi
with
\eta=2
,
|\xi|\leq 20
, and
T=1
, and recorded

\max_{0\leq t\leq T}|h(t,z)|
. For the rough-parameter set

(\alpha,\gamma,\rho,\nu,\theta,V_{0})=(0.62,0.1,-0.681,0.331,0.3156,0.0392),

we obtained

\max_{|\xi|\leq 20}\max_{0\leq t\leq 1}|h(t,2+\mathrm{i}\xi)|\approx 184.2933,

while for the slice-comparison parameter set

(\gamma,\rho,\nu,\theta,V_{0},T)=(0.3,-0.7,1,0.02/0.3,0.02,1),

we obtained

\max_{|\xi|\leq 20}\max_{0\leq t\leq 1}|h(t,2+\mathrm{i}\xi)|\approx 53.3162,\ 54.1052,\ 56.4927,\ 59.0652

for
\alpha=0.55,0.62,0.80,0.95
. These computations support the bounded-strip
Riccati regularity condition for the reported parameter sets only and do not
replace a general proof.

4.3
Rough case
(\alpha=0.62)

We now turn to the rough specification used for the main pricing experiments.
The same FFT-accelerated INAR(
\infty
) simulator from
Subsection
4.1
is used for all payoff classes on

[0,T]
. Here we focus on the numerical evidence most directly tied to the
analytical and modeling claims of the paper.

\tau
. For a European call option with strike

.

, the estimates lie within narrow

\tau
, the benchmark deviation is generally smaller at finer discretizations in this experiment, with the smallest deviation attained at
\tau=320
.

Balancing accuracy and cost, we therefore use
\tau=320
as a practical default:
further increases yield improvements that are within sampling error while the
computational time grows noticeably. We keep
\tau=320
for the remaining experiments
unless otherwise stated.

We now examine the practical effect of choosing a finite discretization level
\tau
. To investigate the empirical accuracy-cost trade-off, we conducted a series of convergence tests for a European call option with strike
K=100
, using an increasing number of time steps from
\tau=40
to
\tau=320
.

4.3.2
Comparison with existing
\alpha<1
schemes on implied-volatility slices

We next consider a fixed-maturity implied-volatility slice and compare our INAR(
\infty
)-based simulator with three representative alternatives from the recent literature in the regime
\alpha<1
. More precisely, we use the rough Heston parameter set

\gamma=0.3,\ \theta=\frac{0.02}{0.3},\ \nu=1,\ \rho=-0.7,\ V_{0}=0.02,\ S_{0}=100,\ T=1,

and the log-moneyness grid
k=\log(K/S_{0})\in\{-0.5,-0.4,\ldots,0.5\}
. We compare the FFT-accelerated INAR(
\infty
) scheme with a hybrid quadratic-exponential (HQE) weak scheme from the affine-forward-variance literature
Gatheral (2022)
;
Bayer and Breneis (2024)
, a multifactor/integrated Euler approximation in the spirit of
Alfonsi and Kebaier (2024)
, and the inverse-Gaussian iVi scheme of
Abi Jaber and Attal (2025)
. For all methods, we use
2^{24}
Monte Carlo paths and a common time discretization of
160
steps; for the multifactor approximation, we use
3
factors. The reference slice is computed by Fourier inversion of the rough Heston characteristic function.

The resulting smile overlays are shown in Figure
1
. In each panel, the overall smile shape is captured well, but the slice-level accuracy differs materially across methods and roughness levels. The corresponding maximum absolute slice errors and wall-clock times are reported in Table
4
.

. In each panel, the black curve is the Fourier reference smile, the red curve is the INAR(
\infty
)-CDQ-FFT slice, the blue and green curves are the iVi and HQE weak-scheme benchmarks, and the brown curve is the
3
-factor integrated multifactor Euler approximation. All methods use

Several conclusions emerge from Figure
1
and Table
4
. First, in this experiment and at the common step budget used here, the specialized weak schemes HQE and iVi produce the smallest European-slice errors for the rougher cases
\alpha=0.55
and
\alpha=0.62
, with HQE delivering the smallest error among the methods tested. Second, under this parameter set, the INAR(
\infty
) approximation becomes more competitive as
\alpha
increases: at
\alpha=0.80
its slice error is close to the iVi/HQE benchmarks, and at
\alpha=0.95
its maximum IV-slice error is slightly smaller than that of HQE and smaller than that of iVi. This is consistent with the refined weak-error decomposition, in which the coefficient of the discrete-to-continuous Volterra branch vanishes as
\alpha\uparrow 1^{-}
. Under the present proof, one obtains at best an
\mathcal{O}(1-\alpha)
vanishing rate, so that for moderate
\tau
the
\tau^{-\alpha}
branch may dominate the observed error. Third, the coarse three-factor integrated approximation is visibly less accurate in this experiment, especially near
\alpha=1
, which is consistent with a deterioration of the underlying low-factor kernel approximation in that regime. More precisely, when
\alpha=0.95
(so
H=\alpha-\tfrac{1}{2}=0.45
), the Volterra kernel is already close to the constant Heston kernel, while the present three-factor approximation still represents it only through a small number of strictly decaying exponential modes; in the absence of a zero-decay mode, this low-factor kernel approximation can become noticeably less accurate in that near-Heston regime.

These slice experiments therefore clarify the numerical position of the proposed scheme. On European implied-volatility slices, the INAR(
\infty
) simulator is not uniformly the most efficient competitor against current specialized weak schemes. However, its slice accuracy improves substantially as the roughness parameter approaches the classical regime, while still relying on the same microstructural simulator that also handles the path-dependent contracts studied above.

(\alpha=1)

\infty
) scheme with the classical Heston benchmark at
\alpha=1
, using the degenerate Markovian kernel from Remark
2.4
.

\infty
) implementation to this benchmark. The results are given in Table
5
. We choose
\tau=320
,
S_{0}=100
and
K=80,90,100,110,120
for all cases.

4.4.1
Classical-Heston benchmark details

The classical Heston benchmark is simulated on a discrete time grid with time step

\Delta t=T/\tau=1/320
. For each path, the asset price
S
and variance
V

are discretized as

\displaystyle S_{i+1}

\displaystyle=S_{i}+rS_{i}\Delta t+\sqrt{\max(V_{i},0)}S_{i}\sqrt{\Delta t}Z_{1},

\displaystyle V_{i+1}

\displaystyle=\max\left(0,V_{i}+\gamma(\theta-V_{i})\Delta t+\nu\sqrt{\max(V_{i},0)}\sqrt{\Delta t}Z_{2}\right),

where
i=0,\dots,\tau-1
, and
(Z_{1},Z_{2})
are correlated standard normal
random variables with correlation
\rho
. This corresponds to a diffusion-truncation
scheme with terminal projection: we use
\sqrt{\max(V_{i},0)}
in the diffusion
term and finally set
V_{i+1}\leftarrow\max\{V_{i+1},0\}
. To reduce Monte Carlo
variance, we employ antithetic variates. For each pair
(Z_{1},Z_{2})
, we simulate
a second path with
(-Z_{1},-Z_{2})
and use the average payoff of the two paths.
For efficiency, the benchmark accumulates path summaries (terminal value,
running average, running extrema, and barrier-hit indicators) on the fly and
prices all strikes/payoffs in a single Monte Carlo pass. All benchmark prices
reported in the following use 500,000 paths, matching the INAR(
\infty
) runs.

The results indicate that, in this experiment, the optimized INAR(
\infty
)
implementation tracks the classical Heston benchmark closely when
\alpha=1
.
European and Asian option prices are numerically close across the two
implementations, and the lookback and barrier prices remain similarly close.

Thus, in the pure Heston regime the specialized Euler benchmark is faster: for the full option set reported above, the optimized Euler–Maruyama benchmark requires about 2.4 seconds, compared with about 13.1 seconds for the optimized INAR(
\infty
) implementation. Beyond this timing comparison, our framework retains the practical advantage of using the same simulator architecture in both rough and classical volatility regimes. This unified treatment is primarily useful when one wants to compare different volatility assumptions within a single implementation framework, rather than to claim a uniform advantage for European pricing in the classical case.

4.5
Analysis of the implied volatility surface

To validate the INAR(
\infty
)-based simulator, we examine the implied-volatility (IV) surface produced by our method. A widely discussed feature in the rough-volatility literature is that rough models generate a steeper short-maturity at-the-money (ATM) skew than classical models, often together with an approximate power-law term structure over some maturity ranges. However, the empirical status of a simple power-law ATM skew in equity markets is more nuanced: recent studies show that such a behavior is not uniformly supported across maturities, and rough models need not outperform non-rough or Markovian alternatives when the entire implied-volatility surface is taken into account; see, for example,
Guyon and El Amrani (2022)
;
Abi Jaber and Li (2025)
. Accordingly, the numerical experiment below should be interpreted as a model-implied diagnostic for the rough Heston specification used in this paper, rather than as evidence that a universal market stylized fact is a pure power law.

Following the simulation setup in Section
4
, we price European calls on a grid of maturities and log-moneyness
k=\log(K/S_{0})\in[-0.2,0.2]
. For each
(T,k)
we invert the Black-Scholes formula to obtain IVs. Figure
2
reports the resulting surface for the rough specification (
\alpha=0.62
), computed with the same path count and time step used throughout the study. We consider maturities on the interval
[0,1]
, sampled at monthly grid points.

Figure 2
:
Implied-volatility surface produced by the roughness specification (
\alpha=0.62
) with the INAR(
\infty
)-FFT simulator. Grid:
T\in\{1/12,2/12,3/12,...,1\}
,
k\in[-0.2,0.2]
. All runs use a fixed time step (
\tau=320
per year) and
10^{6}
paths.

4.5.1
ATM-skew diagnostics

To make this model-implied behavior more explicit, Figure
3
(a) traces the finite-difference ATM skew across maturities, while Figure
3
(b) shows the corresponding ATM implied volatilities. In our rough Heston specification, the skew steepens as maturity shrinks, whereas the ATM level follows a smoother upward term structure. Fitting a power law to the absolute skew (Figure
3
, panel (c)) yields

|\mathrm{skew}(T)|\approx cT^{-0.369}
with
R^{2}\approx 0.850
, which is consistent with the short-end pattern generated by the rough specification with
\alpha=0.62
(so that
H=\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\approx 0.12
). We stress, however, that this is a property of the model under our chosen parameters, not a claim that observed market ATM skews universally follow a pure power law; see
Guyon and El Amrani (2022)
;
Abi Jaber and Li (2025)
for a related empirical discussion.

Figure 3
:
ATM diagnostics under the rough specification (
\alpha=0.62
). Panel (a) shows the steepening skew toward short maturities; panel (b) reports the smoother ATM level term structure; panel (c) illustrates the approximate power-law decay
|\mathrm{skew}(T)|\propto T^{H-1/2}
.

Overall, under the chosen parameter set, the INAR(
\infty
) approximation reproduces the familiar short-maturity rough-volatility patterns within a unified simulation framework. We interpret this as a model-implied diagnostic of the rough specification used here, rather than as evidence of a universal empirical law or of uniform superiority over specialized European-pricing schemes.

5
Conclusion

We introduce a bivariate cumulative heavy-tailed INAR(
\infty
) framework whose nearly unstable scaling limit is the rough Heston model of
El Euch and Rosenbaum (2019)
, extending to the bivariate price–variance setting the discrete cumulative INAR scaling picture developed in
Cai et al. (2026)
. This provides a discrete-time microstructural interpretation of the joint variance-price dynamics and makes the link between the asymmetry of the microscopic order-flow mechanism and the macroscopic leverage effect explicit through a concrete parameter mapping.

On the pricing side, we derived the exact finite-
\tau
transform recursion, reduced it in the diffusive regime to a quadratic discrete Volterra equation, and compared it with the continuous rough Heston fractional Riccati equation. The resulting weak-error analysis is local in frequency: under the admissible-strip assumption stated in Section
3
, we obtain quantitative bounds for the truncated Carr–Madan pricing functional on bounded frequency windows of the form
C_{1}\tau^{-\alpha}+C_{2}(\alpha)\tau^{-(1-\alpha)}
, where
C_{2}(\alpha)\to 0
as
\alpha\uparrow 1^{-}
. This should be viewed as a rigorous low-frequency pricing result rather than as a complete weak-error theory for full European call prices, since the high-frequency tail of the inversion integral is not analyzed in the present paper.

From a computational perspective, the INAR(
\infty
) representation leads to a unified simulation architecture for both European and path-dependent options. The CDQ–FFT implementation reduces the long-memory convolution cost to
\mathcal{O}(\tau\log^{2}\tau)
per path, and the numerical experiments show that the resulting scheme is numerically stable across several payoff classes under the reported parameter sets, remains consistent with the classical Heston benchmark when
\alpha=1
, and reproduces the qualitative implied-volatility features generated by the chosen rough specification. At the same time, the experiments also indicate that on European implied-volatility slices our method should be viewed as complementary to specialized weak schemes rather than as a uniform replacement for them.

Several directions remain open. On the analytical side, it would be desirable to derive the common transform strip directly from the discrete dynamics and to extend the local weak-error result to full Fourier-based European pricing by adding explicit high-frequency tail estimates. On the numerical side, natural next steps include calibration, variance reduction, and statistical inference for the underlying microstructural INAR model, as well as a broader comparison with specialized rough-volatility schemes across different parameter regimes.

Overview

These appendices collect the longer proofs and technical bridge results referenced in the main paper.

Appendix A
Proofs for the Scaling-Limit Section

Throughout Appendices
A
and
B
, we use the following asymptotic notation, with the relevant limiting regime specified by the context. We write
f\sim g
to denote asymptotic equivalence, i.e.,
f/g\to 1
in the stated limit. We use
f\lesssim g
to indicate that
f\leq Cg
for some positive constant
C
independent of the asymptotic parameter in the stated regime. We write
x\asymp y
to denote that there exist constants
0<c<C<\infty

such that
cy\leq x\leq Cy
uniformly in the stated regime.

A.1
Lemma
A.1

Lemma A.1

(A
C
-tightness criterion for càdlàg processes,
(
Horst et al., 2023
, Lemma 3.5)
)
.

If

\sup_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|X_{0}^{(n)}\right|^{q_{0}}\right]<\infty

for some
q_{0}>0
, then for every fixed horizon
T>0
the sequence

\left(X^{(n)}\right)_{n\geq 1}
is
C
-tight on
[0,T]
if there exists a
constant
\vartheta>2
such that the following two conditions hold.

(i)

\sup_{k=0,1,\ldots,\lfloor Tn^{\vartheta}\rfloor}\sup_{h\in[0,1/n^{\vartheta}]}\left|\Delta_{h}X_{k/n^{\vartheta}}^{(n)}\right|\rightarrow 0\quad\text{in probability as }n\rightarrow\infty.

(ii)

There exist constants
C>0
,
p\geq 1
,
m\in\{1,2,\ldots\}
and pairs

\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m}
satisfying

a_{i}\geq 0,\qquad b_{i}>0,\qquad\varrho_{*}:=\min_{1\leq i\leq m}\{b_{i}+a_{i}/\vartheta\}>1,

such that for all
n\geq 1
and
h\in(0,1)
,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\Delta_{h}X_{t}^{(n)}\right|^{p}\right]\leq C\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{h^{b_{i}}}{n^{a_{i}}}.

A.2
Lemma
A.2

Lemma A.2

(Weak convergence of the renewal kernel)
.

Let
(\psi_{n}^{\tau})_{n\geq 1}
be the sequence of discrete renewal kernels associated
with
\varphi^{\tau}=a_{\tau}\varphi
, and define

\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(t):=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{a_{\tau}}\tau\,\psi^{\tau}_{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor},\qquad t\geq 0.

Then, as
\tau\to\infty
, the measure
\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(t)\,dt
converges weakly on

[0,\infty)
to the probability measure with density

f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t):=\gamma t^{\alpha-1}E_{\alpha,\alpha}(-\gamma t^{\alpha}),\qquad t>0.

Proof.

Fix
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
,
\gamma>0
, and
z>0
. Let
(\varphi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a probability kernel on
\mathbb{N}
satisfying

\sum_{n\geq 1}\varphi_{n}=1,\qquad\overline{\varphi}(n):=\sum_{k>n}\varphi_{k}\sim\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}n^{-\alpha}\qquad(n\to\infty).

For each
\tau>0
, let
a_{\tau}\in(0,1)
be such that

\tau^{\alpha}(1-a_{\tau})\to\gamma,

and define

\varphi_{n}^{\tau}:=a_{\tau}\varphi_{n},\qquad n\geq 1.

For
n\geq 1
, let

\psi_{n}^{\tau}:=\sum_{k\geq 1}(\varphi^{\tau})^{*k}_{n}=\sum_{k\geq 1}a_{\tau}^{k}(\varphi^{*k})_{n}

be the discrete renewal kernel associated with
\varphi^{\tau}
, and set

\psi_{0}^{\tau}:=0
.
Define the piecewise-constant function

\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(t):=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{a_{\tau}}\tau\,\psi^{\tau}_{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor},\qquad t\geq 0.

Since
\psi_{0}^{\tau}=0
, this means
\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(t)=0
on
[0,1/\tau)
.

We first check that
\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(t)\,dt
is a probability measure on
[0,\infty)
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(t)\,dt=\sum_{n\geq 0}\int_{n/\tau}^{(n+1)/\tau}\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{a_{\tau}}\tau\,\psi_{n}^{\tau}\,dt=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{a_{\tau}}\sum_{n\geq 0}\psi_{n}^{\tau}=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{a_{\tau}}\sum_{n\geq 1}\psi_{n}^{\tau}.

Since

\sum_{n\geq 1}\psi_{n}^{\tau}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{n\geq 1}(\varphi^{\tau})^{*k}_{n}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\|\varphi^{\tau}\|_{1}^{k}=\sum_{k\geq 1}a_{\tau}^{k}=\frac{a_{\tau}}{1-a_{\tau}},

it follows that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(t)\,dt=1.

Now consider the Laplace transform

\widehat{\mathsf{S}^{\tau}}(z):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-zt}\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(t)\,dt.

Since
\mathsf{S}^{\tau}
is constant on each interval
[n/\tau,(n+1)/\tau)
,
n\geq 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{S}^{\tau}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{a_{\tau}}\tau\sum_{n\geq 0}\psi_{n}^{\tau}\int_{n/\tau}^{(n+1)/\tau}e^{-zt}\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{a_{\tau}}\tau\sum_{n\geq 1}\psi_{n}^{\tau}\frac{e^{-zn/\tau}-e^{-z(n+1)/\tau}}{z}=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{a_{\tau}}\frac{1-e^{-z/\tau}}{z}\tau\sum_{n\geq 1}\psi_{n}^{\tau}e^{-zn/\tau}.

Let

\hat{\varphi}(s):=\sum_{n\geq 1}\varphi_{n}e^{-sn},\qquad s>0.

Then

\sum_{n\geq 1}\psi_{n}^{\tau}e^{-zn/\tau}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(a_{\tau}\hat{\varphi}(z/\tau)\right)^{k}=\frac{a_{\tau}\hat{\varphi}(z/\tau)}{1-a_{\tau}\hat{\varphi}(z/\tau)}.

Therefore,

\widehat{\mathsf{S}^{\tau}}(z)=(1-a_{\tau})\frac{1-e^{-z/\tau}}{z}\tau\,\frac{\hat{\varphi}(z/\tau)}{1-a_{\tau}\hat{\varphi}(z/\tau)}.

(A.1)

Since

\frac{\tau(1-e^{-z/\tau})}{z}\to 1\qquad(\tau\to\infty),

it remains to identify the small-
s
behavior of
\hat{\varphi}(s)
as
s\downarrow 0
.

By Abel summation,

\hat{\varphi}(s)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\varphi_{n}e^{-sn}=1-(1-e^{-s})\sum_{n\geq 0}e^{-sn}\overline{\varphi}(n).

Using the tail asymptotic

\overline{\varphi}(n)\sim\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}n^{-\alpha},

together with the standard discrete Karamata–Tauberian asymptotic

\sum_{n\geq 1}e^{-sn}n^{-\alpha}\sim\Gamma(1-\alpha)s^{\alpha-1}\qquad(s\downarrow 0),

we obtain

(1-e^{-s})\sum_{n\geq 0}e^{-sn}\overline{\varphi}(n)\sim s^{\alpha},\qquad s\downarrow 0.

Hence,

\hat{\varphi}(s)=1-s^{\alpha}+o(s^{\alpha}),\qquad s\downarrow 0.

Substituting
s=z/\tau
, we get

1-a_{\tau}\hat{\varphi}(z/\tau)=(1-a_{\tau})+a_{\tau}(z/\tau)^{\alpha}+o(\tau^{-\alpha}).

Since
(1-a_{\tau})\sim\gamma\tau^{-\alpha}
and
\hat{\varphi}(z/\tau)\to 1
, (
A.1
) yields

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{S}^{\tau}}(z)

\displaystyle\to\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{(1-a_{\tau})\hat{\varphi}(z/\tau)}{1-a_{\tau}\hat{\varphi}(z/\tau)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\frac{(1-a_{\tau})\left(1-(z/\tau)^{\alpha}+o(\tau^{-\alpha})\right)}{(1-a_{\tau})+a_{\tau}(z/\tau)^{\alpha}+o(\tau^{-\alpha})}=\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+z^{\alpha}}.

Now recall the classical Laplace transform identity

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-zt}\,\gamma t^{\alpha-1}E_{\alpha,\alpha}(-\gamma t^{\alpha})\,dt=\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+z^{\alpha}},\qquad z>0.

Thus the pointwise Laplace-transform limit is exactly the Laplace transform of the probability density

f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t):=\gamma t^{\alpha-1}E_{\alpha,\alpha}(-\gamma t^{\alpha}),\qquad t>0.

Since each
\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(t)\,dt
is a probability measure on
[0,\infty)
, the continuity theorem for Laplace transforms implies

\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(t)\,dt\Rightarrow f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t)\,dt\qquad(\tau\to\infty).

This completes the proof.
∎

A.3
Lemma
A.3

For later use, define the discrete cumulative resolvent by

\mathcal{G}_{n}^{\tau}:=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\gamma a_{\tau}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\psi_{k}^{\tau},\qquad n\geq 0,

with
\mathcal{G}_{0}^{\tau}=0
.

Lemma A.3

(Doney-type identity and pointwise resolvent bound)
.

Define

R_{n}^{\tau}:=1_{\{n=0\}}+\psi_{n}^{\tau},\qquad n\geq 0.

Then, for every
n\geq 1
,

nR_{n}^{\tau}=a_{\tau}\sum_{m=1}^{n}m\varphi_{m}(R^{\tau}*R^{\tau})_{n-m}.

(A.2)

Moreover, for every
T>0
, there exists
C_{T}>0
, independent of
\tau
, such
that

\psi_{n}^{\tau}\leq C_{T}n^{\alpha-1},\qquad 1\leq n\leq N_{\tau}.

(A.3)

Equivalently,

\Delta\mathcal{G}_{n}^{\tau}:=\mathcal{G}_{n}^{\tau}-\mathcal{G}_{n-1}^{\tau}=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\gamma a_{\tau}}\psi_{n}^{\tau}\leq C_{T}\tau^{-\alpha}n^{\alpha-1},\qquad 1\leq n\leq N_{\tau}.

(A.4)

Proof.

Let

\mathcal{R}_{\tau}(q):=\sum_{n\geq 0}R_{n}^{\tau}q^{n}=1+\sum_{n\geq 1}\psi_{n}^{\tau}q^{n}=\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}\Phi(q)}.

Differentiating gives

q\mathcal{R}_{\tau}^{\prime}(q)=a_{\tau}\,q\Phi^{\prime}(q)\,\left(\mathcal{R}_{\tau}(q)\right)^{2}.

Since

q\Phi^{\prime}(q)=\sum_{m\geq 1}m\varphi_{m}q^{m},

comparing coefficients of
q^{n}
yields (
A.2
).

Now write
R^{\tau}=\delta_{0}+\psi^{\tau}
, where
\delta_{0}
denotes the unit mass at
0
, i.e.
\delta_{0}(n)=1_{\{n=0\}}
. Expanding (
A.2
) gives

n\psi_{n}^{\tau}=a_{\tau}n\varphi_{n}+2a_{\tau}\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}m\varphi_{m}\,\psi_{n-m}^{\tau}+a_{\tau}\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}m\varphi_{m}\,(\psi^{\tau}*\psi^{\tau})_{n-m}.

(A.5)

Because

\varphi_{n}\asymp n^{-1-\alpha},\qquad m\varphi_{m}\asymp m^{-\alpha},

we prove by induction that
\psi_{j}^{\tau}\leq Cj^{\alpha-1}
for
1\leq j\leq n-1
, with
C
large enough.

Under this induction hypothesis, the convolution
(\psi^{\tau}*\psi^{\tau})_{\ell}\leq C^{2}\sum_{r=1}^{\ell-1}r^{\alpha-1}(\ell-r)^{\alpha-1}
. This is a standard beta-integral convolution: splitting at
\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor
shows both halves contribute
\mathcal{O}(\ell^{2\alpha-1})
, giving

(\psi^{\tau}*\psi^{\tau})_{\ell}\leq C_{1}\ell^{2\alpha-1}.

The linear and quadratic sums in (
A.5
) are then controlled by the same midpoint-split argument. For the linear term
\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}m^{-\alpha}(n-m)^{\alpha-1}
, the two halves give
n^{\alpha-1}\cdot\mathcal{O}(n^{1-\alpha})
and
n^{-\alpha}\cdot\mathcal{O}(n^{\alpha})
, both
\mathcal{O}(1)
. For the quadratic term
\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}m^{-\alpha}(n-m)^{2\alpha-1}
, the two halves give
\mathcal{O}(n^{\alpha})
. Inserting into (
A.5
) yields
n\psi_{n}^{\tau}\leq C_{5}n^{\alpha}
, and hence
\psi_{n}^{\tau}\leq C_{5}n^{\alpha-1}
. Choosing
C\geq C_{5}
closes the induction.
Finally, since

\Delta\mathcal{G}_{n}^{\tau}=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\gamma a_{\tau}}\psi_{n}^{\tau},

(
A.4
) is immediate from (
A.3
).
∎

A.4
Proof of Proposition
2.6

Fix
T>0
.

Step 1: uniform moment bounds for the intensities.

We first establish the moment estimates for the intensities that will be used throughout the proof.

For
1\leq s\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor
, using Definition
2.3
and

1-\sum_{u=1}^{s-1}\varphi_{u}^{\tau}=(1-a_{\tau})+a_{\tau}\sum_{u=s}^{\infty}\varphi_{u},

we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\tau}+\xi_{0}\mu_{\tau}\left(\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}}\left((1-a_{\tau})+a_{\tau}\sum_{u=s}^{\infty}\varphi_{u}\right)-a_{\tau}\sum_{u=1}^{s-1}\varphi_{u}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\mu_{\tau}\left(1+\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}}\sum_{u=s}^{\infty}\varphi_{u}\right).

For the kernel from Definition
2.3
,

\sum_{u=s}^{\infty}\varphi_{u}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}(s-1)^{-\alpha},\qquad s\geq 2,

while
\sum_{u=1}^{\infty}\varphi_{u}=1
. Since

\mu_{\tau}=\mu\tau^{\alpha-1}
and
1-a_{\tau}=\gamma\tau^{-\alpha}
, it follows that

\sup_{1\leq s\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)\lesssim\tau^{2\alpha-1}.

(A.6)

Next fix
n\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor
. By (
2.2
),

\lambda_{n}^{\tau,+}=\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(n)+\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)+\kappa_{n}^{\tau,+},

where

\kappa_{n}^{\tau,+}:=\frac{1}{1+\beta}\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\left(M_{s}^{\tau,+}-M_{s-1}^{\tau,+}+\beta\left(M_{s}^{\tau,-}-M_{s-1}^{\tau,-}\right)\right).

We first bound the deterministic convolution term
\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)
. From the explicit estimate obtained above,

\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)\lesssim\tau^{\alpha-1}+\tau^{2\alpha-1}s^{-\alpha},

uniformly for
1\leq s\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor
. Writing
j=n-s
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)\lesssim\tau^{\alpha-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\psi_{j}^{\tau}+\tau^{2\alpha-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\psi_{j}^{\tau}(n-j)^{-\alpha}.

(A.7)

For the first term on the right side of (
A.7
),

\tau^{\alpha-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\psi_{j}^{\tau}\leq\tau^{\alpha-1}\sum_{j\geq 1}\psi_{j}^{\tau}=\tau^{\alpha-1}\frac{a_{\tau}}{1-a_{\tau}}\lesssim\tau^{2\alpha-1}.

For the second term on the right side of (
A.7
), Lemma
A.3
gives

\psi_{j}^{\tau}\leq C_{T}j^{\alpha-1},\qquad 1\leq j\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor.

Hence,

\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\psi_{j}^{\tau}(n-j)^{-\alpha}\lesssim\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}j^{\alpha-1}(n-j)^{-\alpha}.

We split this sum at
j=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
. For
1\leq j\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor
,
we have
n-j\asymp n
, and thus

\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}j^{\alpha-1}(n-j)^{-\alpha}\lesssim n^{-\alpha}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}j^{\alpha-1}\lesssim n^{-\alpha}n^{\alpha}\lesssim 1.

For
\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1\leq j\leq n-1
, write
r=n-j
. Then
j\asymp n
, so that

\sum_{j=\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}^{n-1}j^{\alpha-1}(n-j)^{-\alpha}\lesssim n^{\alpha-1}\sum_{r=1}^{\lceil n/2\rceil}r^{-\alpha}\lesssim n^{\alpha-1}n^{1-\alpha}\lesssim 1.

Therefore,

\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\psi_{j}^{\tau}(n-j)^{-\alpha}\lesssim 1,

uniformly in
n\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor
. Consequently,

\sup_{1\leq n\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)\lesssim\tau^{2\alpha-1}.

(A.8)

Taking expectations in (
2.2
) and using that
\kappa_{n}^{\tau,+}
has mean zero,
we deduce from (
A.6
) and
(
A.8
) that

\sup_{1\leq n\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[\lambda_{n}^{\tau,+}]\lesssim\tau^{2\alpha-1}.

(A.9)

We next bound the second moment. Since martingale increments at different times are orthogonal,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(\kappa_{n}^{\tau,+}\right)^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{(1+\beta)^{2}}\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}(\psi_{n-s}^{\tau})^{2}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}+\beta^{2}\lambda_{s}^{\tau,-}\right]=\frac{1+\beta^{2}}{(1+\beta)^{2}}\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}(\psi_{n-s}^{\tau})^{2}\mathbb{E}[\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}],

where we used
\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}=\lambda_{s}^{\tau,-}
. Using (
A.9
) and
Lemma
A.3
,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[(\kappa_{n}^{\tau,+})^{2}\right]\lesssim\tau^{2\alpha-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}(\psi_{j}^{\tau})^{2}\lesssim\tau^{2\alpha-1}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}j^{2\alpha-2}\lesssim\tau^{4\alpha-2},

since
2\alpha-2>-1
.

Returning to (
2.2
), the deterministic part

\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(n)+\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)

is
\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2\alpha-1})
uniformly in
n\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor
, and hence

\sup_{1\leq n\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[(\lambda_{n}^{\tau,+})^{2}]\lesssim\tau^{4\alpha-2}.

(A.10)

The same bounds hold for the “
-
” component.

Step 2:
C
-tightness of
\mathbf{Y}^{\tau}
.

We apply Lemma
A.1
componentwise to
(Y^{\tau,+})_{\tau\geq 1}
and

(Y^{\tau,-})_{\tau\geq 1}
. Since
Y_{0}^{\tau,\pm}=0
, the initial-moment condition is trivial.

We check the lemma for
Y^{\tau,+}
; the proof for
Y^{\tau,-}
is identical.

Condition (i).

Fix
\vartheta>2
. Since
Y^{\tau,+}
is piecewise constant on the grid

\{n/\tau:n\geq 0\}
,

\sup_{k=0,\dots,\lfloor T\tau^{\vartheta}\rfloor-1}\sup_{h\in[0,\tau^{-\vartheta}]}\left|\Delta_{h}Y_{k/\tau^{\vartheta}}^{\tau,+}\right|\leq\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\sup_{1\leq s\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}X_{s}^{\tau,+}.

Hence it suffices to show

\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\sup_{1\leq s\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}X_{s}^{\tau,+}\to 0\qquad\text{in probability.}

By the union bound and Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\sup_{1\leq s\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}X_{s}^{\tau,+}>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\frac{\mathbb{E}[(X_{s}^{\tau,+})^{2}]}{\left(\frac{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}{1-a_{\tau}}\varepsilon\right)^{2}}.

Since
X_{s}^{\tau,+}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\sim\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+})
,

\mathbb{E}[(X_{s}^{\tau,+})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}]+\mathbb{E}[(\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+})^{2}].

Using (
A.9
), (
A.10
), and

\frac{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}{1-a_{\tau}}\asymp\tau^{2\alpha},

we obtain

\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\frac{\mathbb{E}[(X_{s}^{\tau,+})^{2}]}{\left(\frac{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}{1-a_{\tau}}\varepsilon\right)^{2}}\lesssim\tau^{-1}\to 0.

Thus condition (i) holds.

Condition (ii).

Take
p=2
and use the pairs

(a_{1},b_{1})=(0,2)
and
(a_{2},b_{2})=(1,1)
.
Then

\varrho_{*}=\min\{2,\,1+1/\vartheta\}>1.

For
t\in[0,T]
and
h\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left|\Delta_{h}Y_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\sum_{i=\lfloor\tau t\rfloor+1}^{\lfloor\tau(t+h)\rfloor}X_{i}^{\tau,+}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\right)^{2}\left(\lfloor\tau(t+h)\rfloor-\lfloor\tau t\rfloor\right)\sum_{i=\lfloor\tau t\rfloor+1}^{\lfloor\tau(t+h)\rfloor}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{i}^{\tau,+}\right)^{2}\right].

Using (
A.9
)–(
A.10
), we have

\sup_{1\leq i\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{i}^{\tau,+}\right)^{2}\right]\lesssim\tau^{4\alpha-2},

and therefore

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left|\Delta_{h}Y_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|^{2}\right]\lesssim\left(\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}}\right)^{2}(\tau h+1)^{2}\tau^{4\alpha-2}\lesssim h^{2}+\frac{h}{\tau}.

Hence condition (ii) of Lemma
A.1
holds. Thus

(Y^{\tau,+})_{\tau\geq 1}
is
C
-tight. The same proof applies to

(Y^{\tau,-})_{\tau\geq 1}
, so
\mathbf{Y}^{\tau}
is
C
-tight.

Step 3:
C
-tightness of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\tau}
.

By Lemma
2.7
,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{t}^{\tau}-\mathbf{Y}_{t}^{\tau}\|_{1}\to 0\qquad\text{in probability.}

Since
\mathbf{Y}^{\tau}
is
C
-tight, it follows that
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\tau}
is also

C
-tight.

Step 4:
C
-tightness of
\mathbf{Z}^{\tau}
.

We verify the
C
-tightness of
Z^{\tau,+}
and
Z^{\tau,-}
via a
martingale tightness criterion (e.g.
(
Rebolledo, 1980
, Proposition 12)
):
it suffices to show that their predictable quadratic variation processes are

C
-tight and that their jumps vanish in probability.

By definition,

Z_{t}^{\tau,+}=\sqrt{\frac{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}{1-a_{\tau}}}\left(Y_{t}^{\tau,+}-\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,+}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}}\,M_{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor}^{\tau,+},

and similarly

Z_{t}^{\tau,-}=\sqrt{\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}}\,M_{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor}^{\tau,-}.

Hence
Z^{\tau,+}
and
Z^{\tau,-}
are square-integrable càdlàg martingales. Moreover,

\langle Z^{\tau,+},Z^{\tau,+}\rangle_{t}=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor}\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}=\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,+},

and similarly

\langle Z^{\tau,-},Z^{\tau,-}\rangle_{t}=\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,-}.

Since
(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\tau})_{\tau\geq 1}
is
C
-tight, both sequences

(\langle Z^{\tau,+},Z^{\tau,+}\rangle)_{\tau\geq 1}
and

(\langle Z^{\tau,-},Z^{\tau,-}\rangle)_{\tau\geq 1}
are
C
-tight.
It remains to show that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\Delta Z_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0,\qquad\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\Delta Z_{t}^{\tau,-}\right|\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0.

Since

\Delta Z_{t}^{\tau,+}=\sqrt{\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}}\left(M_{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor}^{\tau,+}-M_{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}^{\tau,+}\right),

we have

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\Delta Z_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|\leq\sqrt{\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}}\sup_{1\leq s\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\left|X_{s}^{\tau,+}-\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}\right|.

We use the fourth-moment Markov inequality. For every
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\Delta Z_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\!\left(\left|X_{s}^{\tau,+}-\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}\right|>\varepsilon\sqrt{\frac{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}{1-a_{\tau}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\frac{\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left|X_{s}^{\tau,+}-\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}\right|^{4}\right]}{\varepsilon^{4}\left(\frac{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}{1-a_{\tau}}\right)^{2}}.

Now conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{s-1}
,

X_{s}^{\tau,+}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\sim\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}),

and the centered fourth moment of a Poisson random variable is

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(X_{s}^{\tau,+}-\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}\right)^{4}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\right]=\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}+3(\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+})^{2}.

Taking expectations and using (
A.9
) and (
A.10
),
we obtain

\sup_{1\leq s\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left|X_{s}^{\tau,+}-\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}\right|^{4}\right]\lesssim\tau^{2\alpha-1}+\tau^{4\alpha-2}\lesssim\tau^{4\alpha-2}.

Since

\left(\frac{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}{1-a_{\tau}}\right)^{2}\asymp\tau^{4\alpha},

it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\!\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\Delta Z_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\lfloor\tau T\rfloor\cdot\tau^{4\alpha-2}\cdot\tau^{-4\alpha}\lesssim\tau^{-1}\to 0.

Thus,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\Delta Z_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|\to 0\qquad\text{in probability.}

The same proof applies to
Z^{\tau,-}
.

Therefore, by
(
Rebolledo, 1980
, Proposition 12)
,

(Z^{\tau,+})_{\tau\geq 1}
and
(Z^{\tau,-})_{\tau\geq 1}
are
C
-tight.
Hence
(\mathbf{Z}^{\tau})_{\tau\geq 1}
is
C
-tight on
[0,T]
. Moreover,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|\Delta\mathbf{Z}_{t}^{\tau}\right\|_{1}\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\Delta Z_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\Delta Z_{t}^{\tau,-}\right|\to 0

in probability.

Step 5: identification of the quadratic variation of any limit point.

Let
(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
be a limit point of
(\mathbf{Y}^{\tau},\mathbf{Z}^{\tau})
.

For the “
+
” component,

[Z^{\tau,+},Z^{\tau,+}]_{n/\tau}=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\sum_{s=1}^{n}(X_{s}^{\tau,+}-\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+})^{2},

so that

U_{n}^{\tau,+}:=[Z^{\tau,+},Z^{\tau,+}]_{n/\tau}-\langle Z^{\tau,+},Z^{\tau,+}\rangle_{n/\tau}=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\sum_{s=1}^{n}\left((X_{s}^{\tau,+}-\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+})^{2}-\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}\right).

Then
(U_{n}^{\tau,+})_{n\geq 0}
is a martingale, and by orthogonality of martingale
increments,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(U_{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor}^{\tau,+}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\right)^{2}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathrm{Var}\!\left((X_{s}^{\tau,+}-\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+})^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\right)\right].

Since
X_{s}^{\tau,+}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\sim\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+})
,

\mathrm{Var}\!\left((X_{s}^{\tau,+}-\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+})^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\right)=2(\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+})^{2}+\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}.

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(U_{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor}^{\tau,+}\right)^{2}\right]\lesssim\left(\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\right)^{2}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor}\left(\mathbb{E}[(\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+})^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}]\right).

Using (
A.9
) and (
A.10
), we get

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(U_{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor}^{\tau,+}\right)^{2}\right]\lesssim\tau^{-1},

uniformly for
t\in[0,T]
. Hence, by Doob’s inequality,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|[Z^{\tau,+},Z^{\tau,+}]_{t}-\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|\to 0\qquad\text{in probability.}

Combining this with Lemma
2.7
,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|[Z^{\tau,+},Z^{\tau,+}]_{t}-Y_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|\to 0\qquad\text{in probability.}

Passing to the limit yields

[Z^{+},Z^{+}]_{t}=Y_{t}^{+}.

The same argument gives

[Z^{-},Z^{-}]_{t}=Y_{t}^{-}.

For the cross-variation,

\left[Z^{\tau,+},Z^{\tau,-}\right]_{n/\tau}=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\sum_{s=1}^{n}\left(X_{s}^{\tau,+}-\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}\right)\left(X_{s}^{\tau,-}-\lambda_{s}^{\tau,-}\right).

Because
X_{s}^{\tau,+}
and
X_{s}^{\tau,-}
are conditionally independent given

\mathcal{F}_{s-1}
, the process

V_{n}^{\tau}:=[Z^{\tau,+},Z^{\tau,-}]_{n/\tau}

is a martingale. Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(V_{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor}^{\tau}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\right)^{2}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}\lambda_{s}^{\tau,-}\right].

Since
\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}=\lambda_{s}^{\tau,-}
and (
A.10
) holds,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(V_{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor}^{\tau}\right)^{2}\right]\lesssim\tau^{-1}.

Hence, by Doob’s inequality,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|[Z^{\tau,+},Z^{\tau,-}]_{t}|\to 0\qquad\text{in probability,}

and therefore

[Z^{+},Z^{-}]_{t}=0.

Since
(\mathbf{Z}^{\tau})_{\tau\geq 1}
is
C
-tight, every limit point
\mathbf{Z}
is continuous.
Therefore
\mathbf{Z}
is a continuous martingale with

[\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z}]=\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{Y}).

This completes the proof.

A.5
Proof of Lemma
2.7

Fix
T>0
. It suffices to prove

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,+}-Y_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|\to 0\qquad\text{in probability as }\tau\to\infty,

since the proof for the “
-
” component is identical.

Since

Y_{t}^{\tau,+}-\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,+}=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\,M_{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor}^{\tau,+},

Doob’s
L^{2}
inequality yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|Y_{t}^{\tau,+}-\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,+}|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq n\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}|M_{n}^{\tau,+}|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[|M_{\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}^{\tau,+}|^{2}\right].

Because
M^{\tau,+}
is a square-integrable martingale,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|M_{\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}^{\tau,+}\right|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle M^{\tau,+}\right\rangle_{\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\right]=\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}].

Using the uniform estimate (
A.9
), we have

\sup_{1\leq s\leq\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}]\lesssim\tau^{2\alpha-1},

and therefore

\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau T\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[\lambda_{s}^{\tau,+}]\lesssim\tau^{2\alpha}.

Hence,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|Y_{t}^{\tau,+}-\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|^{2}\right]\lesssim\left(\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}\right)^{2}\tau^{2\alpha}\lesssim\tau^{-2\alpha}\to 0.

Thus,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|Y_{t}^{\tau,+}-\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|\to 0\qquad\text{in }L^{2},

and hence also in probability. This completes the proof.

A.6
Proof of Proposition
2.8

Fix
T>0
. Let

\left(Y^{\tau_{k},+},Y^{\tau_{k},-},Z^{\tau_{k},+},Z^{\tau_{k},-}\right)

be a subsequence converging in law on
D([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{4})
to a limit point

(Y^{+},Y^{-},Z^{+},Z^{-}).

By Proposition
2.6
, every limit point is continuous. Hence, by the
Skorokhod representation theorem, after possibly enlarging the probability space
and still denoting the subsequence by
\tau_{k}
, we may assume that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\left|Y_{t}^{\tau_{k},+}-Y_{t}^{+}\right|+\left|Y_{t}^{\tau_{k},-}-Y_{t}^{-}\right|+\left|Z_{t}^{\tau_{k},+}-Z_{t}^{+}\right|+\left|Z_{t}^{\tau_{k},-}-Z_{t}^{-}\right|\right)\to 0\qquad\text{a.s.}

(A.11)

as
k\to\infty
.

Step 1: the two limit components of
\mathbf{Y}^{\tau}
coincide.

Recall that

\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,+}=\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,-},\qquad t\in[0,T].

Hence, by Lemma
2.7
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|Y_{t}^{\tau,+}-Y_{t}^{\tau,-}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|Y_{t}^{\tau,+}-\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,+}\right|+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|Y_{t}^{\tau,-}-\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,-}\right|

\displaystyle\to 0\qquad\text{in probability.}

Passing to the almost sure Skorokhod realization along the chosen subsequence,
we obtain

Y_{t}^{+}=Y_{t}^{-}\qquad\text{for all }t\in[0,T],\ \text{a.s.}

We therefore write

Y_{t}:=Y_{t}^{+}=Y_{t}^{-},\qquad t\in[0,T].

Step 2: an explicit decomposition for
\Lambda^{\tau,+}
.

We first show that for every integer
n\geq 1
,

\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(n)+\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)=\mu_{\tau}+\xi_{0}\mu_{\tau}\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}}+\mu_{\tau}(1-\xi_{0})\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}.

(A.12)

Indeed, Definition
2.3
gives

\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(n)=\mu_{\tau}+\xi_{0}\mu_{\tau}\left(\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}}\left(1-\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}\right)-\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}\right).

Hence,

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(n)+\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\tau}+\xi_{0}\mu_{\tau}\left(\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}}\left(1-\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}\right)-\sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\mu_{\tau}\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}+\xi_{0}\mu_{\tau}\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\left(\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}}\left(1-\sum_{r=1}^{s-1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}\right)-\sum_{r=1}^{s-1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}\right).

Since

1-\sum_{r=1}^{s-1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}=1-\sum_{r\geq 1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}+\sum_{r=s}^{\infty}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}=(1-a_{\tau})+\sum_{r=s}^{\infty}\varphi_{r}^{\tau},

we obtain

\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}}\left(1-\sum_{r=1}^{s-1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}\right)-\sum_{r=1}^{s-1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}=1+\left(1+\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}}\right)\sum_{r=s}^{\infty}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}-\sum_{r\geq 1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}.

Since
\sum_{r\geq 1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}=a_{\tau}
, this becomes

\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}}\left(1-\sum_{r=1}^{s-1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}\right)-\sum_{r=1}^{s-1}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}=(1-a_{\tau})+\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}}\sum_{r=s}^{\infty}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}+\sum_{r=s}^{\infty}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}.

To compute the convolution term explicitly, we rewrite
\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)
in terms of the tail

\sum_{r=s}^{\infty}\varphi_{r}^{\tau}
and then use the renewal identity

\psi^{\tau}=\varphi^{\tau}+\psi^{\tau}*\varphi^{\tau},

which implies, for every
n\geq 1
,

\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\varphi_{s}^{\tau}=\psi_{n}^{\tau}-\varphi_{n}^{\tau}.

Using this identity inside the convolution term above and rearranging yields

\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)=\xi_{0}\mu_{\tau}\left(\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}}+1\right)\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\varphi_{n-s}^{\tau}+\mu_{\tau}(1-\xi_{0})\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}.

Substituting this back into the expression for

\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(n)+\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)
gives
(
A.12
).

Now recall from (
2.2
) that

\lambda_{n}^{\tau,+}=\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(n)+\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(s)+\frac{1}{1+\beta}\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\left(\Delta M_{s}^{\tau,+}+\beta\Delta M_{s}^{\tau,-}\right),

where

\Delta M_{s}^{\tau,\pm}:=M_{s}^{\tau,\pm}-M_{s-1}^{\tau,\pm}.

Using (
A.12
), we obtain

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}^{\tau,+}

\displaystyle=\mu_{\tau}+\mu_{\tau}\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}+\xi_{0}\mu_{\tau}\left(\frac{1}{1-a_{\tau}}-\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{1+\beta}\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\left(\Delta M_{s}^{\tau,+}+\beta\Delta M_{s}^{\tau,-}\right).

(A.13)

We now sum (
A.13
) over
n=1,\dots,m
.
For the deterministic term, Tonelli’s theorem gives

\sum_{n=1}^{m}\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}=\sum_{s=1}^{m-1}\sum_{j=1}^{m-s}\psi_{j}^{\tau}=\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}(m-j)\psi_{j}^{\tau}=\sum_{s=1}^{m-1}s\psi_{m-s}^{\tau}.

For the martingale term, define

H_{s}^{\tau}:=M_{s}^{\tau,+}+\beta M_{s}^{\tau,-},\qquad\Delta H_{s}^{\tau}:=H_{s}^{\tau}-H_{s-1}^{\tau}.

Then

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{m}\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\Delta H_{s}^{\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{m-1}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m-s}\psi_{j}^{\tau}\right)\Delta H_{s}^{\tau}.

Set

A_{s}^{\tau}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m-s}\psi_{j}^{\tau},\qquad 1\leq s\leq m-1.

By discrete summation by parts,

\sum_{s=1}^{m-1}A_{s}^{\tau}\Delta H_{s}^{\tau}=A_{m-1}^{\tau}H_{m-1}^{\tau}+\sum_{s=1}^{m-2}(A_{s}^{\tau}-A_{s+1}^{\tau})H_{s}^{\tau}.

Since

A_{s}^{\tau}-A_{s+1}^{\tau}=\psi_{m-s}^{\tau},\qquad A_{m-1}^{\tau}=\psi_{1}^{\tau},

this yields

\sum_{n=1}^{m}\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\psi_{n-s}^{\tau}\Delta H_{s}^{\tau}=\sum_{s=1}^{m-1}\psi_{m-s}^{\tau}H_{s}^{\tau}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{m}\lambda_{n}^{\tau,+}

\displaystyle=\mu_{\tau}m+\mu_{\tau}\sum_{s=1}^{m-1}s\psi_{m-s}^{\tau}+\xi_{0}\mu_{\tau}\left(\frac{m}{1-a_{\tau}}-\sum_{s=1}^{m-1}s\psi_{m-s}^{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{1+\beta}\sum_{s=1}^{m-1}\psi_{m-s}^{\tau}\left(M_{s}^{\tau,+}+\beta M_{s}^{\tau,-}\right).

(A.14)

Taking
m=\lfloor\tau t\rfloor
and multiplying by

\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}
, we obtain

\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,+}=\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,-}=T_{1}^{\tau}(t)+T_{2}^{\tau}(t)+T_{3}^{\tau}(t),

(A.15)

where

\displaystyle T_{1}^{\tau}(t)

\displaystyle:=(1-a_{\tau})\frac{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor}{\tau},

\displaystyle T_{2}^{\tau}(t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}s\psi_{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-s}^{\tau}+\xi_{0}\left(\frac{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor}{\tau}-\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}s\psi_{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-s}^{\tau}\right),

\displaystyle T_{3}^{\tau}(t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu(1+\beta)}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}\psi_{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-s}^{\tau}\left(M_{s}^{\tau,+}+\beta M_{s}^{\tau,-}\right).

Using

Z_{s/\tau}^{\tau,\pm}=\sqrt{\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}}\,M_{s}^{\tau,\pm},

we can rewrite
T_{3}^{\tau}
as

T_{3}^{\tau}(t)=\frac{\tau(1-a_{\tau})}{\sqrt{\gamma\mu(1+\beta)^{2}}}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}\frac{1}{\tau}\psi_{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-s}^{\tau}\left(Z_{s/\tau}^{\tau,+}+\beta Z_{s/\tau}^{\tau,-}\right).

(A.16)

Step 3: passage to the limit in the decomposition.

Recall the rescaled renewal density

\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(u):=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{a_{\tau}}\tau\psi_{\lfloor\tau u\rfloor}^{\tau},\qquad u\geq 0,

and Lemma
A.2
, which states that

\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(u)\,du\Rightarrow f_{\alpha,\gamma}(u)\,du

weakly on
[0,\infty)
, where

f_{\alpha,\gamma}(u):=\gamma u^{\alpha-1}E_{\alpha,\alpha}(-\gamma u^{\alpha})

and

F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(u)\,du
.

Fix
t\in[0,T]
. We identify the limits of the three terms in
(
A.15
).

First,

T_{1}^{\tau}(t)=(1-a_{\tau})\frac{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor}{\tau}\to 0,

since
1-a_{\tau}=\gamma\tau^{-\alpha}\to 0
.

Next, writing
j=\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-s
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}s\psi_{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-s}^{\tau}=\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}(\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-j)\psi_{j}^{\tau}=\frac{a_{\tau}}{\tau}\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(j/\tau)\left(\frac{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor}{\tau}-\frac{j}{\tau}\right).

Since the function

u\mapsto(t-u)\mathbf{1}_{[0,t]}(u)

is bounded and continuous on
[0,\infty)
, the weak convergence of

\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(u)\,du
implies

\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}s\psi_{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-s}^{\tau}\to\int_{0}^{t}(t-u)f_{\alpha,\gamma}(u)\,du=\int_{0}^{t}F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\,ds.

Also,

\frac{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor}{\tau}\to t
.
Hence,

T_{2}^{\tau}(t)\to\int_{0}^{t}F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\,ds+\xi_{0}\left(t-\int_{0}^{t}F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\,ds\right).

(A.17)

We now treat
T_{3}^{\tau}(t)
. Using (
A.16
), write

\displaystyle T_{3}^{\tau}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{\tau}}{\sqrt{\gamma\mu(1+\beta)^{2}}}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}\frac{1}{\tau}\mathsf{S}^{\tau}\!\left(\frac{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-s}{\tau}\right)\left(Z_{s/\tau}^{\tau,+}+\beta Z_{s/\tau}^{\tau,-}\right).

Set

G^{\tau}(s):=Z_{s}^{\tau,+}+\beta Z_{s}^{\tau,-},\qquad G(s):=Z_{s}^{+}+\beta Z_{s}^{-}.

Then

\displaystyle T_{3}^{\tau}(t)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma\mu(1+\beta)^{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)G(s)\,ds

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{\tau}}{\sqrt{\gamma\mu(1+\beta)^{2}}}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}\frac{1}{\tau}\mathsf{S}^{\tau}\!\left(\frac{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-s}{\tau}\right)\left(G^{\tau}(s/\tau)-G(s/\tau)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma\mu(1+\beta)^{2}}}\left[a_{\tau}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}\frac{1}{\tau}\mathsf{S}^{\tau}\!\left(\frac{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-s}{\tau}\right)G(s/\tau)-\int_{0}^{t}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)G(s)\,ds\right]

\displaystyle=:I_{1}^{\tau}(t)+I_{2}^{\tau}(t).

For the first term, since
a_{\tau}\leq 1
and

\frac{1}{\tau}\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-1}\mathsf{S}^{\tau}\!\left(\frac{\lfloor\tau t\rfloor-s}{\tau}\right)\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(u)\,du=1,

we get

|I_{1}^{\tau}(t)|\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma\mu(1+\beta)^{2}}}\sup_{0\leq r\leq T}|G^{\tau}(r)-G(r)|\to 0

almost surely by (
A.11
).

For the second term, the function

u\mapsto G(t-u)\mathbf{1}_{[0,t]}(u)

is bounded and continuous on
[0,\infty)
because
G
is continuous. Hence the weak
convergence of
\mathsf{S}^{\tau}(u)\,du
implies that

I_{2}^{\tau}(t)\to 0
.
Therefore,

T_{3}^{\tau}(t)\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma\mu(1+\beta)^{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\left(Z_{s}^{+}+\beta Z_{s}^{-}\right)\,ds.

(A.18)

Combining (
A.15
), (
A.17
),
(
A.18
), and Lemma
2.7
, which yields

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\Lambda_{t}^{\tau,+}-Y_{t}^{\tau,+}|\to 0\qquad\text{in probability},

we conclude that for every fixed
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle Y_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\,ds+\xi_{0}\left(t-\int_{0}^{t}F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\,ds\right)

(A.19)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma\mu(1+\beta)^{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\left(Z_{s}^{+}+\beta Z_{s}^{-}\right)\,ds.

Step 4:
Y
is absolutely continuous and its density.

Define

c_{\beta,\gamma,\mu}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma\mu(1+\beta)^{2}}},\qquad H_{t}:=Z_{t}^{+}+\beta Z_{t}^{-}.

Then (
A.19
) becomes

Y_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\,ds+\xi_{0}\left(t-\int_{0}^{t}F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\,ds\right)+c_{\beta,\gamma,\mu}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)H_{s}\,ds.

Since
H
is a continuous semimartingale and
F_{\alpha,\gamma}(0)=0
,
integration by parts yields

\int_{0}^{t}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)H_{s}\,ds=\int_{0}^{t}F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\,dH_{s}.

Now
f_{\alpha,\gamma}\in L^{2}([0,T])
because
\alpha>1/2
, and by
Proposition
2.6
,

[H]_{t}=[Z^{+}+\beta Z^{-}]_{t}=(1+\beta^{2})Y_{t},

which is finite on
[0,T]
. Hence,

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)^{2}\,d[H]_{s}\,dt<\infty\qquad\text{a.s.},

so that stochastic Fubini’s theorem applies. Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\,dH_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{s}^{t}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(r-s)\,dr\,dH_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{r}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(r-s)\,dH_{s}\,dr.

Substituting this into the previous display, we obtain

Y_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}v_{s}\,ds,

where

v_{t}=\xi_{0}\left(1-F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t)\right)+F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t)+c_{\beta,\gamma,\mu}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\,dH_{s}.

(A.20)

Step 5: Brownian representation of
Z^{+}
and
Z^{-}
.

By Proposition
2.6
, every limit point satisfies

[Z^{+},Z^{+}]_{t}=Y_{t},\qquad[Z^{-},Z^{-}]_{t}=Y_{t},\qquad[Z^{+},Z^{-}]_{t}=0.

Since
Y_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}v_{s}\,ds
is absolutely continuous, the quadratic variations
of
Z^{+}
and
Z^{-}
are absolutely continuous. Hence, by the representation theorem
for continuous local martingales with absolutely continuous quadratic variation,
there exist standard Brownian motions
B^{1}
and
B^{2}
such that

Z_{t}^{+}=\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{v_{s}}\,dB_{s}^{1},\qquad Z_{t}^{-}=\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{v_{s}}\,dB_{s}^{2}.

Moreover, since
[Z^{+},Z^{-}]\equiv 0
, the two-dimensional Brownian motion

(B^{1},B^{2})
has covariance matrix equal to the identity, and therefore

B^{1}
and
B^{2}
are independent.

Thus

dH_{s}=d(Z_{s}^{+}+\beta Z_{s}^{-})=\sqrt{v_{s}}\,\left(dB_{s}^{1}+\beta\,dB_{s}^{2}\right).

Define

W_{t}^{V}:=\frac{B_{t}^{1}+\beta B_{t}^{2}}{\sqrt{1+\beta^{2}}}.

Then
W^{V}
is a standard Brownian motion, and

dH_{s}=\sqrt{1+\beta^{2}}\,\sqrt{v_{s}}\,dW_{s}^{V}.

Substituting this into (
A.20
), we obtain

v_{t}=\xi_{0}\left(1-F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t)\right)+F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t)+\sqrt{\frac{1+\beta^{2}}{\gamma\mu(1+\beta)^{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\sqrt{v_{s}}\,dW_{s}^{V}.

Therefore

Y_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}v_{s}\,ds,\qquad Z_{t}^{+}=\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{v_{s}}\,dB_{s}^{1},\qquad Z_{t}^{-}=\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{v_{s}}\,dB_{s}^{2},

with

v_{t}=\xi_{0}\left(1-F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t)\right)+F_{\alpha,\gamma}(t)+\sqrt{\frac{1+\beta^{2}}{\gamma\mu(1+\beta)^{2}}}\int_{0}^{t}f_{\alpha,\gamma}(t-s)\sqrt{v_{s}}\,dW_{s}^{V}.

This proves the claimed representation. The Hölder regularity of
v

follows from Proposition 6.2 of
El Euch and Rosenbaum (2019)
. This completes
the proof of Proposition
2.8
.

A.7
Proof of Theorem
2.9

From Proposition
2.6
, we have obtained
C
-tightness of
(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\tau},\mathbf{Y}^{\tau},\mathbf{Z}^{\tau})_{\tau\geq 1}
, so every subsequence admits a further subsequence converging in law to some limit point
(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})
. The same proposition also identifies the limiting quadratic covariation:

[\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z}]=\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{Y}),\qquad[Z^{+},Z^{-}]_{t}=0.

Proposition
2.8
then shows that every such limit point admits a Brownian representation with independent components and that its rate process
v
, defined by
Y_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}v_{s}\,ds
, satisfies the stochastic Volterra equation (
2.5
). Rewriting that equation in the rough Heston form (
2.6
) and invoking the well-posedness and uniqueness results for the rough Heston variance equation from
El Euch and Rosenbaum (2019)
identifies the limit uniquely.

Hence all subsequential limit points coincide, and the whole sequence converges in law to the unique limit described in Theorem
2.9
. This completes the proof of Theorem
2.9
.

A.8
Proof of Theorem
2.10

The proof follows the logic of Corollary 2.1 of
El Euch and Rosenbaum (2019)
, leveraging the convergence result established in our Theorem
2.9
and applying the continuous mapping theorem. We outline the key steps.

First, recall the definition of the properly rescaled microscopic price process
P_{t}^{\tau}
:

\displaystyle{P_{t}^{\tau}}={\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}}\left(N_{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor}^{\tau,+}-N_{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor}^{\tau,-}\right)-\frac{\theta}{2}\frac{1-a_{\tau}}{\tau^{\alpha}\mu}N_{\lfloor t\tau\rfloor}^{\tau,+}}={\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{2}}\left(Z_{t}^{\tau,+}-Z_{t}^{\tau,-}\right)-\frac{\theta}{2}Y_{t}^{\tau,+}.}

The mapping from the process triplet
(Z_{t}^{\tau,+},Z_{t}^{\tau,-},Y_{t}^{\tau,+})
to
P_{t}^{\tau}
is a continuous linear combination. Theorem
2.9
establishes the joint convergence in law of these processes to their limits
(Z_{t}^{+},Z_{t}^{-},Y_{t}^{+})
in the Skorokhod topology. By the continuous mapping theorem,
P_{t}^{\tau}
converges in law to a process
P_{t}
given by the same linear combination of the limits:

\displaystyle P_{t}={\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{2}}(Z_{t}^{+}-Z_{t}^{-})-\frac{\theta}{2}Y_{t}^{+}}=\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{v_{s}}dB_{s}^{1}-\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{v_{s}}dB_{s}^{2}\right)-\frac{\theta}{2}\int_{0}^{t}v_{s}ds,

where we have substituted the integral representations of the limit processes from Theorem
2.9
.

Now define the variance process
V_{t}:=\theta v_{t}
and the Brownian motion

W_{t}^{X}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(B_{t}^{1}-B_{t}^{2}).

Then

\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{2}}\left(dB_{t}^{1}-dB_{t}^{2}\right)=\sqrt{\theta}\,dW_{t}^{X},

so that

\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{v_{s}}\,(dB_{s}^{1}-dB_{s}^{2})=\sqrt{\theta}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{v_{s}}\,dW_{s}^{X}=\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\theta v_{s}}\,dW_{s}^{X}=\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{V_{s}}\,dW_{s}^{X}.

Hence,

P_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{V_{s}}dW_{s}^{X}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}V_{s}ds.

This is precisely the log-price process in the rough Heston model under the risk-neutral measure.

To obtain the SDE for the variance process
V_{t}
, we simply multiply the SDE for
v_{t}
from Theorem
2.9
by the constant
\theta
. This yields equation (
2.11
).

Finally, we compute the correlation between the Brownian motions
W^{X}
and
W^{V}
. Using the properties of quadratic covariation and the definitions of
W_{t}^{X}
and
W_{t}^{V}=(B_{t}^{1}+\beta B_{t}^{2})/\sqrt{1+\beta^{2}}
, we have:

\displaystyle d\left\langle W^{X},W^{V}\right\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=d\left\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(B^{1}-B^{2}\right),\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\beta^{2}}}\left(B^{1}+\beta B^{2}\right)\right\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2(1+\beta^{2})}}\left(d\left\langle B^{1},B^{1}\right\rangle_{t}+\beta d\left\langle B^{1},B^{2}\right\rangle_{t}-d\left\langle B^{2},B^{1}\right\rangle_{t}-\beta d\left\langle B^{2},B^{2}\right\rangle_{t}\right).

Since
B^{1}
and
B^{2}
are independent standard Brownian motions, we have
d\langle B^{1},B^{1}\rangle_{t}=d\langle B^{2},B^{2}\rangle_{t}=dt
and
d\langle B^{1},B^{2}\rangle_{t}=0
. This simplifies to:

d\left\langle W^{X},W^{V}\right\rangle_{t}=\frac{1-\beta}{\sqrt{2(1+\beta^{2})}}dt.

This completes the proof.

Appendix B
Technical Results for the Weak-Error Analysis

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
f\in C^{\alpha}([0,T])
, we define

[f]_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in[0,T]\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{|x-y|^{\alpha}},

(B.1)

and

\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}:=\|f\|_{\infty}+[f]_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}.

(B.2)

B.1
Proof of Proposition
3.1

Proof.

We use the explicit form of
\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(m)
from Definition
2.3
. Write

\overline{\varphi}(n):=\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}\varphi_{k},\qquad n\geq 0.

For the kernel chosen in Definition
2.3
, a direct telescoping computation gives

\overline{\varphi}(0)=1,\qquad\overline{\varphi}(n)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}n^{-\alpha},\qquad n\geq 1.

(B.3)

Moreover,

1-\sum_{s=1}^{m-1}\varphi_{s}^{\tau}=1-a_{\tau}\sum_{s=1}^{m-1}\varphi_{s}=\varepsilon_{\tau}+a_{\tau}\overline{\varphi}(m-1).

Substituting this into the definition of
\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(m)
yields

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(m)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\tau}+\xi_{0}\mu_{\tau}\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{\tau}+a_{\tau}\overline{\varphi}(m-1)}{\varepsilon_{\tau}}-a_{\tau}\sum_{s=1}^{m-1}\varphi_{s}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\tau}+\xi_{0}\mu_{\tau}\varepsilon_{\tau}+\xi_{0}\mu_{\tau}a_{\tau}\left(\varepsilon_{\tau}^{-1}+1\right)\overline{\varphi}(m-1).

(B.4)

Multiplying by
\varepsilon_{\tau}/\mu
and using
\mu_{\tau}=\mu\tau^{\alpha-1}
and
\varepsilon_{\tau}=\gamma\tau^{-\alpha}
, we obtain

\frac{\varepsilon_{\tau}}{\mu}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(m)=\frac{\gamma}{\tau}+\frac{\xi_{0}\gamma^{2}}{\tau^{\alpha+1}}+\xi_{0}a_{\tau}\tau^{\alpha-1}\overline{\varphi}(m-1)+\frac{\xi_{0}\gamma a_{\tau}}{\tau}\overline{\varphi}(m-1).

(B.5)

Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon_{\tau}}{\mu}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(m)f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma}{\tau}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)+\xi_{0}a_{\tau}\tau^{\alpha-1}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\overline{\varphi}(m-1)f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)+R_{\tau}(f),

(B.6)

where, by (
B.3
),

\displaystyle|R_{\tau}(f)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{\infty}\left(\tau^{-\alpha}+\frac{1}{\tau}\sum_{m=2}^{N_{\tau}}(m-1)^{-\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{\infty}\left(\tau^{-\alpha}+\tau^{-1}N_{\tau}^{1-\alpha}\right)\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{\infty}\tau^{-\alpha}.

(B.7)

We now treat the two main terms in (
B.6
).

Step 1: regular Riemann sum.

Set

x_{m}:=T-\frac{m}{\tau},\qquad m=1,\dots,N_{\tau}.

Then

\frac{1}{\tau}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)=\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\int_{x_{m}}^{x_{m}+1/\tau}f(x_{m})\,ds.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\tau}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)-\int_{0}^{T}f(s)\,ds\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\int_{x_{m}}^{x_{m}+1/\tau}|f(x_{m})-f(s)|\,ds+\left|\int_{N_{\tau}/\tau}^{T}f(s)\,ds\right|.

Since
f\in C^{\alpha}([0,T])
, by (
B.1
), for

s\in[x_{m},x_{m}+1/\tau]
,

|f(x_{m})-f(s)|\leq[f]_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}|s-x_{m}|^{\alpha}\leq[f]_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-\alpha},

and hence,

\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\int_{x_{m}}^{x_{m}+1/\tau}|f(x_{m})-f(s)|\,ds\leq N_{\tau}\cdot\tau^{-1-\alpha}[f]_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-\alpha}.

Also, since
0\leq T-N_{\tau}/\tau\leq 1/\tau
,

\left|\int_{N_{\tau}/\tau}^{T}f(s)\,ds\right|\leq\frac{1}{\tau}\|f\|_{\infty}\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-1}\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-\alpha},

because
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Thus

\left|\frac{\gamma}{\tau}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)-\gamma\int_{0}^{T}f(s)\,ds\right|\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-\alpha}.

(B.8)

Step 2: weakly singular Riemann sum.

Set

g(u):=f(T-u),\qquad u\in[0,T].

Then
g\in C^{\alpha}([0,T])
and

\|g\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\leq C\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}.

By (
B.3
), the term
m=1
must be treated separately, since

\overline{\varphi}(0)=1,\qquad\overline{\varphi}(j)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}j^{-\alpha},\qquad j\geq 1.

Hence,

\displaystyle\tau^{\alpha-1}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\overline{\varphi}(m-1)f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)=\tau^{\alpha-1}g\!\left(\frac{1}{\tau}\right)+\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\frac{1}{\tau}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\tau}-1}\left(\frac{j}{\tau}\right)^{-\alpha}g\!\left(\frac{j+1}{\tau}\right).

(B.9)

We compare this with

I^{1-\alpha}f(T)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{T}u^{-\alpha}g(u)\,du.

We split the error into the first cell and the remaining cells.

First cell.

Write

\displaystyle\Delta_{0,\tau}

\displaystyle:=\tau^{\alpha-1}g\!\left(\frac{1}{\tau}\right)-\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{1/\tau}u^{-\alpha}g(u)\,du.

Using

g\!\left(\frac{1}{\tau}\right)=g(0)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\tau^{-\alpha}\|g\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\right),

we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{0,\tau}

\displaystyle=g(0)\left[\tau^{\alpha-1}-\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\int_{0}^{1/\tau}u^{-\alpha}\,du\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(\tau^{-1}\|g\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\right).

Since

\int_{0}^{1/\tau}u^{-\alpha}\,du=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\tau^{-(1-\alpha)}

and

\Gamma(2-\alpha)=(1-\alpha)\Gamma(1-\alpha)
,
we can rewrite the bracket as

\tau^{-(1-\alpha)}\left(1-\frac{1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\right).

Therefore,

\Delta_{0,\tau}=\left(1-\frac{1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\right)\tau^{-(1-\alpha)}g(0)+\mathcal{O}\left(\tau^{-1}\|g\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\right).

(B.10)

Since
\alpha\in(1/2,1)
, we have
\tau^{-1}\leq\tau^{-\alpha}
for large
\tau
. Moreover,

1-\frac{1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\to 0

as
\alpha\uparrow 1^{-}
.

Remaining cells.

For
1\leq j\leq N_{\tau}-1
, set

E_{j}:=\left|\frac{1}{\tau}\left(\frac{j}{\tau}\right)^{-\alpha}g\!\left(\frac{j+1}{\tau}\right)-\int_{j/\tau}^{(j+1)/\tau}u^{-\alpha}g(u)\,du\right|.

Then

\displaystyle E_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\frac{1}{\tau}\left(\frac{j}{\tau}\right)^{-\alpha}g\!\left(\frac{j+1}{\tau}\right)-g\!\left(\frac{j+1}{\tau}\right)\int_{j/\tau}^{(j+1)/\tau}u^{-\alpha}\,du\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{j/\tau}^{(j+1)/\tau}u^{-\alpha}\left|g\!\left(\frac{j+1}{\tau}\right)-g(u)\right|\,du.

Using the mean-value theorem for
u^{-\alpha}
and the
\alpha
-Hölder continuity of
g
,

E_{j}\leq C_{T}\|g\|_{\infty}\tau^{\alpha-2}j^{-\alpha-1}+C_{T}\|g\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-1}j^{-\alpha}.

Summing over
1\leq j\leq N_{\tau}-1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\tau}-1}E_{j}

\displaystyle\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\left(\tau^{\alpha-2}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\tau}-1}j^{-\alpha-1}+\tau^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\tau}-1}j^{-\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\left(\tau^{\alpha-2}+\tau^{-1}N_{\tau}^{1-\alpha}\right)\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-\alpha}.

(B.11)

The terminal interval
[N_{\tau}/\tau,T]
has length at most
\tau^{-1}
, so

\int_{N_{\tau}/\tau}^{T}u^{-\alpha}|g(u)|\,du\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{\infty}\tau^{-1}\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-\alpha}.

Combining (
B.10
), (
B.11
), and the tail estimate gives

\displaystyle\left|\tau^{\alpha-1}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\overline{\varphi}(m-1)f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)-I^{1-\alpha}f(T)\right|

(B.12)

\displaystyle\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-\alpha}+C_{T}\left|1-\frac{1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\right|\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-(1-\alpha)}.

It remains to reinsert the prefactor
a_{\tau}
. First,

\displaystyle\tau^{\alpha-1}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\overline{\varphi}(m-1)

\displaystyle=\tau^{\alpha-1}\left(1+\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\tau}-1}j^{-\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{T}\left(\tau^{\alpha-1}+\tau^{\alpha-1}N_{\tau}^{1-\alpha}\right)\leq C_{T}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left|\xi_{0}a_{\tau}\tau^{\alpha-1}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\overline{\varphi}(m-1)f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)-\xi_{0}\tau^{\alpha-1}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\overline{\varphi}(m-1)f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq|\xi_{0}|\,|a_{\tau}-1|\,\tau^{\alpha-1}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\overline{\varphi}(m-1)\left|f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)\right|\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{\infty}\tau^{-\alpha}.

Together with (
B.12
), this yields

\displaystyle\left|\xi_{0}a_{\tau}\tau^{\alpha-1}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\overline{\varphi}(m-1)f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)-\xi_{0}I^{1-\alpha}f(T)\right|

(B.13)

\displaystyle\leq C_{T}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-\alpha}+C_{T}\left|1-\frac{1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\right|\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-(1-\alpha)}.

Finally, combining (
B.6
), (
B.7
),
(
B.8
), and (
B.13
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\varepsilon_{\tau}}{\mu}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(m)f\!\left(T-\frac{m}{\tau}\right)-\left[\gamma\int_{0}^{T}f(s)\,ds+\xi_{0}I^{1-\alpha}f(T)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{T}\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-\alpha}+C_{\mathrm{base}}(\alpha)\|f\|_{C^{\alpha}([0,T])}\tau^{-(1-\alpha)},

with

C_{\mathrm{base}}(\alpha):=C_{T}\left|1-\frac{1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\right|.

In particular,

C_{\mathrm{base}}(\alpha)\to 0

as
\alpha\uparrow 1^{-}
.
This proves (
3.1
).
∎

B.2
Proof of Lemma
3.2

Proof.

Write

a_{n}:=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\frac{1}{n^{\alpha}},\qquad n\geq 1.

From Definition
2.3
, for
n\geq 2
,

\varphi_{n}=a_{n-1}-a_{n}

and
\varphi_{1}=1-a_{1}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 2}\varphi_{n}e^{-sn}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 2}(a_{n-1}-a_{n})e^{-sn}

\displaystyle=e^{-s}\sum_{m\geq 1}a_{m}e^{-sm}-\sum_{n\geq 2}a_{n}e^{-sn}=-\left(1-e^{-s}\right)\sum_{m\geq 1}a_{m}e^{-sm}+a_{1}e^{-s}.

Adding
\varphi_{1}e^{-s}=(1-a_{1})e^{-s}
gives the exact identity

\hat{\varphi}(s)=e^{-s}-\left(1-e^{-s}\right)A(s),\qquad A(s):=\sum_{m\geq 1}a_{m}e^{-sm}=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\mathrm{Li}_{\alpha}(e^{-s}),

where
\mathrm{Li}_{\alpha}
is the polylogarithm. For
0<\alpha<1
, the classical expansion

Olver et al. (2023)
yields

\mathrm{Li}_{\alpha}(e^{-s})=\Gamma(1-\alpha)s^{\alpha-1}+\zeta(\alpha)+\mathcal{O}(s),\qquad s\downarrow 0.

Therefore

A(s)=s^{\alpha-1}+\frac{\zeta(\alpha)}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}+\mathcal{O}(s),

and, since

1-e^{-s}=s-\frac{s^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(s^{3}),\qquad e^{-s}=1-s+\frac{s^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(s^{3}),

we obtain

\displaystyle 1-\hat{\varphi}(s)=1-e^{-s}+\left(1-e^{-s}\right)A(s)=s^{\alpha}+\left(1+\frac{\zeta(\alpha)}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\right)s+\mathcal{O}(s^{1+\alpha})+\mathcal{O}(s^{2}).

Because
1+\alpha<2
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, the
\mathcal{O}(s^{2})
term is absorbed into
\mathcal{O}(s^{1+\alpha})
. This proves (
3.2
), with
c_{1}(\alpha)
given by (
3.3
).

Finally, as
\alpha\uparrow 1^{-}
, we have

\zeta(\alpha)\sim-\frac{1}{1-\alpha}
,

\Gamma(1-\alpha)\sim\frac{1}{1-\alpha}
,
and therefore

\frac{\zeta(\alpha)}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}\to-1
.
This proves the limit statement in Lemma
3.2
.
∎

B.3
Proof of Proposition
3.7

We use the branching–immigration representation of the Poisson INAR(
\infty
) process.

Fix
z\in\mathcal{D}_{\tau}(T)
. Consider first a single “
+
”-immigrant born at the current time. Its direct contribution to
zP_{T}^{\tau}
is
\Theta_{+}^{\tau}(z)
, hence the factor
e^{\Theta_{+}^{\tau}(z)}
.

Now fix a lag
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Because
q_{k}^{\tau,+}
is the lag-
k
coefficient associated with a “
+
”-parent, the numbers of “
+
”-children and “
-
”-children produced at lag
k
are independent Poisson random variables, both with mean
q_{k}^{\tau,+}
. Each such child starts an independent subcluster with remaining horizon
n-k
. Therefore, by the Poisson generating-function identity

\mathbb{E}\left[s^{\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda)}\right]=e^{\lambda(s-1)}
,
the lag-
k
continuation factor equals

\exp\!\left(q_{k}^{\tau,+}(\mathcal{M}_{n-k}^{\tau,+}(z)-1)\right)\,\exp\!\left(q_{k}^{\tau,+}(\mathcal{M}_{n-k}^{\tau,-}(z)-1)\right)=\exp\!\left(q_{k}^{\tau,+}G_{n-k}^{\tau}(z)\right).

Multiplying these independent continuation factors over
k=1,\dots,n
, and including the ancestor contribution
e^{\Theta_{+}^{\tau}(z)}
, we obtain

\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\tau,+}(z)=e^{\Theta_{+}^{\tau}(z)}\prod_{k=1}^{n}\exp\!\left(q_{k}^{\tau,+}G_{n-k}^{\tau}(z)\right)=\exp\!\left(\Theta_{+}^{\tau}(z)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}q_{k}^{\tau,+}G_{n-k}^{\tau}(z)\right),

which is exactly (
3.12
).

The proof of (
3.13
) is identical. For a single “
-
”-immigrant, the lag-
k
numbers of “
+
”- and “
-
”-children are independent Poisson random variables with common mean
q_{k}^{\tau,-}
, so the lag-
k
continuation factor is

\exp\!\left(q_{k}^{\tau,-}(\mathcal{M}_{n-k}^{\tau,+}(z)-1)\right)\,\exp\!\left(q_{k}^{\tau,-}(\mathcal{M}_{n-k}^{\tau,-}(z)-1)\right)=\exp\!\left(q_{k}^{\tau,-}G_{n-k}^{\tau}(z)\right),

which gives (
3.13
).

Now define
G_{n}^{\tau}(z):=\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\tau,+}(z)+\mathcal{M}_{n}^{\tau,-}(z)-2
. Summing (
3.12
) and (
3.13
) and subtracting
2
yields the closed recursion (
3.15
).

Finally, at each time
m\in\{1,\dots,N_{\tau}\}
, the numbers of “
+
”- and “
-
”-immigrants are independent Poisson random variables with common mean
\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(m)
, and clusters generated by distinct immigrants are independent. Hence, again by the Poisson generating-function identity,

\Phi^{\tau}(z,T)=\prod_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\exp\!\left(\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(m)\left(\mathcal{M}_{N_{\tau}-m}^{\tau,+}(z)-1\right)\right)\exp\!\left(\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(m)\left(\mathcal{M}_{N_{\tau}-m}^{\tau,-}(z)-1\right)\right).

Combining the two exponential factors and using the definition of
G_{n}^{\tau}(z)
, we obtain

\Phi^{\tau}(z,T)=\exp\!\left(\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\tau}}\hat{\mu}_{\tau}(m)\,\left(\mathcal{M}_{N_{\tau}-m}^{\tau,+}(z)+\mathcal{M}_{N_{\tau}-m}^{\tau,-}(z)-2\right)\right),

which is exactly (
3.16
). This completes the proof.

B.4
Uniform strip smallness

Proposition B.1

(Uniform strip smallness)
.

For every fixed
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
and
R>0
, there exist
\tau_{0}\geq 1
and a
constant
C_{\eta,R,T}>0
such that for all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
,
with
\varepsilon_{\tau}:=1-a_{\tau}=\gamma\tau^{-\alpha}
,

\sup_{0\leq n\leq N_{\tau}}\sup_{z\in\Gamma_{\eta,R}}|G_{n}^{\tau}(z)|\leq C_{\eta,R,T}\,\varepsilon_{\tau},

(B.14)

and

\sup_{0\leq n\leq N_{\tau}}\sup_{z\in\Gamma_{\eta,R}}\left(|A_{n}^{\tau}(z)|+|B_{n}^{\tau}(z)|\right)\leq C_{\eta,R,T}\,\varepsilon_{\tau}.

(B.15)

In particular, after possibly enlarging the constant
C_{\eta,R,T}
,

\sup_{0\leq n\leq N_{\tau}}\sup_{z\in\Gamma_{\eta,R}}\left(|S_{n}^{\tau}(z)|+|D_{n}^{\tau}(z)|\right)\leq C_{\eta,R,T}\,\varepsilon_{\tau}.

(B.16)

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
.

Step 1: the case
n=0
.

By definition,

A_{0}^{\tau}(z)=\Theta_{+}^{\tau}(z),\qquad B_{0}^{\tau}(z)=\Theta_{-}^{\tau}(z),

hence

|A_{0}^{\tau}(z)|+|B_{0}^{\tau}(z)|\leq|z|\,(2c_{\tau}+d_{\tau})\leq C\varepsilon_{\tau}

uniformly on
\Gamma_{\eta,R}
, because
c_{\tau}\asymp\varepsilon_{\tau}
and

d_{\tau}\asymp\varepsilon_{\tau}^{2}
.

Since
A_{0}^{\tau}(z),B_{0}^{\tau}(z)\to 0
uniformly on
\Gamma_{\eta,R}
, the Taylor expansion gives

e^{A_{0}^{\tau}(z)}+e^{B_{0}^{\tau}(z)}-2=A_{0}^{\tau}(z)+B_{0}^{\tau}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\left((A_{0}^{\tau}(z))^{2}+(B_{0}^{\tau}(z))^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\tau}^{3}),

uniformly on
\Gamma_{\eta,R}
. Therefore,

\sup_{z\in\Gamma_{\eta,R}}|G_{0}^{\tau}(z)|\leq C\varepsilon_{\tau}.

This proves (
B.14
) and (
B.15
) at
n=0
.

Step 2: an analytic estimate for the nonlinear map.

Define

F(a,b):=e^{a}+e^{b}-2.

Since
F
is analytic near
(0,0)
and

F(a,b)=a+b+\frac{1}{2}(a^{2}+b^{2})+O(|a|^{3}+|b|^{3}),

there exist constants
\delta>0
and
C_{\delta}>0
such that whenever

|a|+|b|\leq\delta
,

|F(a,b)|\leq|a+b|+C_{\delta}\left(|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}\right).

(B.17)

Step 3: induction step.

Assume that for some
n\geq 1
,

\sup_{0\leq j\leq n-1}\sup_{z\in\Gamma_{\eta,R}}|G_{j}^{\tau}(z)|\leq M\varepsilon_{\tau}

(B.18)

for some constant
M>0
to be fixed later.

Then from (
3.17
),

\displaystyle|A_{n}^{\tau}(z)|\leq|\Theta_{+}^{\tau}(z)|+\sum_{k=1}^{n}q_{k}^{\tau,+}|G_{n-k}^{\tau}(z)|\leq M_{\eta,R}(c_{\tau}+d_{\tau})+\frac{a_{\tau}}{1+\beta}M\varepsilon_{\tau}\leq C\varepsilon_{\tau},

and similarly

\displaystyle|B_{n}^{\tau}(z)|\leq|\Theta_{-}^{\tau}(z)|+\sum_{k=1}^{n}q_{k}^{\tau,-}|G_{n-k}^{\tau}(z)|\leq M_{\eta,R}c_{\tau}+\frac{\beta a_{\tau}}{1+\beta}M\varepsilon_{\tau}\leq C\varepsilon_{\tau}.

Hence

\sup_{z\in\Gamma_{\eta,R}}\left(|A_{n}^{\tau}(z)|+|B_{n}^{\tau}(z)|\right)\leq C\varepsilon_{\tau}.

After enlarging
\tau_{0}
if necessary, we may suppose that

|A_{n}^{\tau}(z)|+|B_{n}^{\tau}(z)|\leq\delta

for all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
, all
0\leq n\leq N_{\tau}
, and all
z\in\Gamma_{\eta,R}
, so that
(
B.17
) applies.

Now use (
B.17
) and (
3.18
):

|G_{n}^{\tau}(z)|\leq|A_{n}^{\tau}(z)+B_{n}^{\tau}(z)|+C\left(|A_{n}^{\tau}(z)|^{2}+|B_{n}^{\tau}(z)|^{2}\right).

Since

A_{n}^{\tau}(z)+B_{n}^{\tau}(z)=S_{n}^{\tau}(z)=-zd_{\tau}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\varphi_{k}^{\tau}\,G_{n-k}^{\tau}(z),

we obtain

\displaystyle|A_{n}^{\tau}(z)+B_{n}^{\tau}(z)|\leq M_{\eta,R}d_{\tau}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\varphi_{k}^{\tau}\,|G_{n-k}^{\tau}(z)|\leq C\varepsilon_{\tau}^{2}+a_{\tau}M\varepsilon_{\tau}.

Moreover, because
A_{n}^{\tau}(z),B_{n}^{\tau}(z)=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\tau})
uniformly,

|A_{n}^{\tau}(z)|^{2}+|B_{n}^{\tau}(z)|^{2}\leq C\varepsilon_{\tau}^{2}.

Hence,

|G_{n}^{\tau}(z)|\leq a_{\tau}M\varepsilon_{\tau}+C\varepsilon_{\tau}^{2}=\left((1-\varepsilon_{\tau})M+C\varepsilon_{\tau}\right)\varepsilon_{\tau}.

Choose
M\geq C
. Then

(1-\varepsilon_{\tau})M+C\varepsilon_{\tau}\leq M
,
so that

|G_{n}^{\tau}(z)|\leq M\varepsilon_{\tau}.

This closes the induction and proves (
B.14
).

Finally, (
B.15
) follows from the bounds on
A_{n}^{\tau},B_{n}^{\tau}
obtained
above. The bound (
B.16
) then follows, after possibly enlarging the
constant, from

S_{n}^{\tau}=A_{n}^{\tau}+B_{n}^{\tau},\qquad D_{n}^{\tau}=A_{n}^{\tau}-B_{n}^{\tau}.

∎

B.5
Proof of Proposition
3.8

Set

U_{n}^{\tau}(z):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\varphi_{k}^{\tau}\,G_{n-k}^{\tau}(z).

Then

A_{n}^{\tau}(z)=z(c_{\tau}-d_{\tau})+\frac{1}{1+\beta}U_{n}^{\tau}(z),\qquad B_{n}^{\tau}(z)=-zc_{\tau}+\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}U_{n}^{\tau}(z).

By Proposition
B.1
, the quantities
A_{n}^{\tau}(z)
and
B_{n}^{\tau}(z)

are uniformly
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\tau})
on
\{0,\dots,N_{\tau}\}\times\Gamma_{\eta,R}
.
Hence, for
\tau
large enough, they belong to a fixed complex neighborhood of the origin on
which the third-order Taylor formula for the exponential map is uniform:

e^{x}=1+x+\frac{x^{2}}{2}+\rho(x),\qquad|\rho(x)|\leq C|x|^{3}.

Applying this with
x=A_{n}^{\tau}(z)
and
x=B_{n}^{\tau}(z)
, and subtracting
2
, yields
